<?xml version="1.0" encoding="UTF-8"?><rss xmlns:atom="http://www.w3.org/2005/Atom" xmlns:content="http://purl.org/rss/1.0/modules/content/" xmlns:dc="http://purl.org/dc/elements/1.1/" xmlns:sy="http://purl.org/rss/1.0/modules/syndication/" version="2.0" xmlns:media="http://search.yahoo.com/mrss/"><channel><title><![CDATA[The National]]></title><link>https://www.thenationalnews.com</link><atom:link href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/arc/outboundfeeds/rss/" rel="self" type="application/rss+xml"/><description><![CDATA[The National News Feed]]></description><lastBuildDate>Fri, 18 Sep 2026 19:21:25 +0000</lastBuildDate><language>en</language><ttl>1</ttl><sy:updatePeriod>hourly</sy:updatePeriod><sy:updateFrequency>1</sy:updateFrequency><item><title><![CDATA[Early voting begins in some states ahead of crucial US midterms]]></title><link>https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/us/2026/09/18/early-voting-begins-in-some-states-ahead-of-crucial-us-midterms/</link><guid isPermaLink="true">https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/us/2026/09/18/early-voting-begins-in-some-states-ahead-of-crucial-us-midterms/</guid><dc:creator><![CDATA[The National]]></dc:creator><description></description><pubDate>Fri, 18 Sep 2026 18:24:02 +0000</pubDate><content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>Early voting began in some US states on Friday, weeks ahead of the <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/tags/us-midterm-elections" target="_self" rel="" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/tags/us-midterm-elections">midterm elections</a> that will determine whether the Democrats can regain power in Congress.</p><p>The issues weighing on the minds of voters include how President <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/tags/donald-trump">Donald Trump</a> is running the country, rising prices for petrol, food and other necessities, and the Iran war.</p><p>Virginia has opened polls in some areas for those wishing to cast their votes early. Voters in some other states, including Idaho, Minnesota and South Dakota, can apply for and cast absentee ballots in person in some places. </p><p>Electoral laws on early voting vary from state to state, with some not opening availability until mid-October. </p><p>The midterm elections, scheduled for November 3, will be pivotal in determining the direction of the final two years of Mr Trump's term as well as that of Congress. Currently, <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/tags/republicans">Republicans</a> maintain control of the White House and Congress, but this is likely to shift following the midterms.</p><p>Races in states such as Texas, Georgia and Michigan are being closely watched. In Michigan, Muslim American Democrat <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/us/2026/08/05/abdul-el-sayed-michigan/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/us/2026/08/05/abdul-el-sayed-michigan/">Abdul El Sayed</a> is running against Trump-endorsed Mike Rogers. And progressive former teacher James Talarico is taking on Republican Ken Paxton in conservative Texas.</p><p>The president's party typically loses congressional seats in the midterms.</p><p>Mr Trump's approval rating is hovering around 35 per cent, according to the latest Reuters/Ipsos poll. </p>]]></content:encoded><media:content url="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/PZODGJFLC4XABXUWRX4O46KSNA.jpg?auth=9eb417b9294cd334fa4a18521dcad65f5a9cd0c64d29b257a5443f93031a6af8&amp;smart=true&amp;width=4107&amp;height=2738" type="image/jpeg" height="2738" width="4107"><media:description type="plain"><![CDATA[Voters cast their ballots inside the Arlington County Courthouse on September 18, 2026 in Arlington, Virginia.  Getty Images via AFP]]></media:description><media:credit role="author" scheme="urn:ebu">TOM BRENNER</media:credit></media:content></item><item><title><![CDATA[UAE Property: 'My landlord is selling my apartment. Can the new owner make me move out?']]></title><link>https://www.thenationalnews.com/business/money/2026/09/18/uae-property-my-landlord-is-selling-my-apartment-can-the-new-owner-make-me-move-out/</link><guid isPermaLink="true">https://www.thenationalnews.com/business/money/2026/09/18/uae-property-my-landlord-is-selling-my-apartment-can-the-new-owner-make-me-move-out/</guid><dc:creator><![CDATA[Mario Volpi]]></dc:creator><description></description><pubDate>Fri, 18 Sep 2026 18:02:00 +0000</pubDate><content:encoded><![CDATA[<p><div style= "float:left;padding-top:5px;padding-right:10px"><img height="100%" src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/usDfKtkBM7kvKIu9np1YqOZ1klM=/arc-photo-thenational/eu-central-1-prod/public/3UAS2HPPXBBKJMXXDRPEWDKFWE.jpg" width="120" /></div></p><p><b>Question: </b>I've rented the same apartment for almost four years and have built up a really good relationship with my landlord. Last week, he told me he's thinking of selling because he feels the market has performed well over the past few years. I'm now worried that if somebody buys the apartment, they'll immediately ask me to move out, even though I'd really like to stay. I've heard so many different opinions from friends that I don't know what's actually true. Could you explain what normally happens when a rented property is sold? <i><b>RW, Downtown Dubai</b></i></p><p><b>Answer: </b>This is a question I'm hearing much more often lately, and fortunately, the answer is usually far less worrying than many tenants imagine.</p><p>The first thing to remember is that selling a property doesn't automatically end <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/business/property/2026/02/25/dubai-rents-to-stop-rising-by-end-of-year-after-half-decade-boom/" target="_blank" rel="" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/business/property/2026/02/25/dubai-rents-to-stop-rising-by-end-of-year-after-half-decade-boom/">the tenancy</a>. When somebody buys a tenanted property, they also take over the existing tenancy agreement. In other words, the lease continues under exactly the same terms until it expires.</p><p>Where people become confused is around vacant possession. If the new owner genuinely intends to move into the property themselves, or in some cases wishes to have a close family member occupy it, there are legal procedures that must be followed. It isn't simply a case of completing the purchase and asking the tenant to leave the following week. The reality is that if the new owner needs you to vacate, they have to give you 12 months' notice to do this. This notice must be sent officially via notary public and/or registered mail. Any other form of communication of this notice would be deemed not delivered correctly.</p><p>Many investors actively look for properties with good tenants already in place because rental income starts from day one. For an investor, there's a great deal of comfort in knowing the property is already occupied by someone who pays their rent and looks after the home.</p><p>If I were in your position, I'd simply keep communicating with your landlord and ask whether he's marketing the apartment as a tenanted investment or with vacant possession in mind. You'll often find that a straightforward conversation removes a great deal of unnecessary anxiety.</p><p>For now, I'd continue enjoying your home and avoid assuming that a sale automatically means you'll have to start packing boxes.</p><p><b>Q: </b>I've spent nearly 15 years building my savings and finally have around Dh3 million that I'd like to invest in Dubai property. Originally, I had my heart set on buying one luxury apartment in a prime location, but the more I read, the more I wonder whether buying two smaller apartments in different communities might actually be a wiser decision. I don't necessarily need the rental income today, but I'd like to build something that will still be performing well 10 or 15 years from now. I'd appreciate your advice on how experienced investors usually approach this. <i><b>RP, Abu Dhabi</b></i></p><p><b>A: </b>I like this question because you're already thinking like an investor rather than simply a buyer. Over the years I've seen people build very successful property portfolios using both approaches. Some have bought one exceptional asset and held it for many years. Others have gradually built a collection of smaller properties that together produced a very healthy income and spread their risk across different market segments and locations.</p><p>Whenever somebody asks me this question, I find myself thinking about flexibility. Owning two properties gives you options. If one community temporarily underperforms or takes longer to rent, the other may continue producing income. If you decide to release some capital in the future, you can sell one property while keeping the other. That flexibility can become extremely valuable over time.</p><p>On the other hand, there are certain locations in Dubai where exceptional properties remain highly sought after regardless of the wider market. Buying quality has never been a bad long-term strategy.</p><p>Rather than deciding purely on the number of properties, I'd spend time looking at the communities themselves. Ask yourself where people will genuinely want to live in 10 years' time, do the research and due diligence. Ask yourself, are schools being built? Is transport getting better? Is employment nearby? Is there enough retail and leisure to support a growing population?</p><p>Good investing rarely comes from chasing the highest rental yield or the most fashionable address. It comes from buying quality property in locations where demand is likely to remain strong long after today's headlines have disappeared.</p><p><i>The opinions expressed do not constitute legal advice and are provided for information only. Please send any questions to </i><a href="mailto:mario@evadxb.com" target="_blank" rel="" title="mailto:mario@evadxb.com"><i>mario@evadxb.com</i></a></p>]]></content:encoded><media:content url="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/5HFXHKIZXNFC7IN6VMU5HIUZQI.jpg?auth=1995da6c750cb1ee05fe8cf0e9165059b9e6b02e6cff891897e79df11539adca&amp;smart=true&amp;width=4256&amp;height=2832" type="image/jpeg" height="2832" width="4256"><media:description type="plain"><![CDATA[Many investors actively look for properties with good tenants already in place because rental income starts from day one. AFP]]></media:description><media:credit role="author" scheme="urn:ebu">EYAD BABA</media:credit></media:content></item><item><title><![CDATA['Rizq': The Arabic word for sustenance encompasses things that money can't buy]]></title><link>https://www.thenationalnews.com/arts-culture/2026/09/18/learn-arabic-rizq-sustenance/</link><guid isPermaLink="true">https://www.thenationalnews.com/arts-culture/2026/09/18/learn-arabic-rizq-sustenance/</guid><dc:creator><![CDATA[Faisal Al Zaabi]]></dc:creator><description></description><pubDate>Fri, 18 Sep 2026 18:01:00 +0000</pubDate><content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>There are some things in life that can be counted easily: a salary, a meal, a house or the money in a bank account. Rizq, however, is a broader idea.</p><p>Our <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/arts-culture/2026/09/11/watan-the-arabic-word-for-homeland-describes-more-than-a-place/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/arts-culture/2026/09/11/watan-the-arabic-word-for-homeland-describes-more-than-a-place/">Arabic word of the week</a> is most commonly translated as sustenance, provision or livelihood, but it can describe much more than material wealth. Rizq may be income or food, but it can also be health, knowledge, family, opportunity or simply the right thing arriving at the right time.</p><p>It comes from the Arabic roots raa, zaa and qaf, associated with providing or granting sustenance. The verb razaqa means “to provide”, while Al Razzaq, meaning “the Provider” or “the Sustainer”, is one of the names of God in <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/tags/islam">Islam</a>.</p><p>That religious dimension is central to how the word is understood. Rizq is often spoken of as something granted by God, which is why the word can carry ideas of both effort and destiny.</p><p>A person may work hard in search of a livelihood, while still believing that the outcome is not entirely in their hands.</p><p>This is reflected in expressions such as talab al-rizq, meaning the “pursuit of a livelihood”, and bab al-rizq, or a “door of provision”, which can refer to a source of work or opportunity.</p><p>Someone starting a new job or finding an unexpected opportunity might describe it as a new bab al-rizq opening for them.</p><p>Another common idea is that rizq is written or already allotted. The expression does not necessarily suggest passivity. Rather, it reflects the belief that a person should make an effort while accepting that not every result can be controlled.</p><p>The word also features naturally in music because of that tension between work, fortune and faith. Egyptian singer Medhat Saleh's <i>Al Rizq</i>, written by Bahaa El Din Mohamed, presents provision as something that extends beyond money and cannot be measured only by what a person earns.</p><iframe width="560" height="315" src="https://www.youtube.com/embed/jAM9FSdFZ6M?feature=oembed" frameborder="0" allow="accelerometer; autoplay; clipboard-write; encrypted-media; gyroscope; picture-in-picture; web-share" referrerpolicy="strict-origin-when-cross-origin" allowfullscreen title="Medhat Saleh - Al Rizq Official lyric video | مدحت صالح - الرزق"></iframe><p>In everyday speech, Allah yerzuqak, meaning “may God provide for you”, can be used as a wish for prosperity or well-being. It may refer to employment, marriage, children or other forms of good fortune.</p><p>The concept is also embedded in names and institutions. Rizq and Rizk are both found as family names across the Arab world, while the word is frequently used by businesses and charitable organisations because of its associations with provision and abundance.</p><p>In Abu Dhabi, <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/arts-culture/2026/06/17/under-the-same-sky-at-rizq-art-initiative-explores-the-many-ways-artists-call-the-uae-home/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/arts-culture/2026/06/17/under-the-same-sky-at-rizq-art-initiative-explores-the-many-ways-artists-call-the-uae-home/">Rizq Art Initiative</a> in Al Reem Island adopts the word in a cultural context, connecting the idea of provision with creativity, artistic opportunity and exchange.</p><p>That flexibility explains why rizq is difficult to capture with a single English equivalent.</p><p>It can mean the salary that supports a family, but also a friendship, an education, a chance encounter or a period of good health. It describes what sustains a person, whether it can be counted or not. </p>]]></content:encoded><media:content url="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/ZWBUHJSMY5HGLMTXBYKDPQDXF4.jpg?auth=0574e5ef232287680fc048dad74c51105ec14819ed747fc0b0230a4d34524c0b&amp;smart=true&amp;width=1260&amp;height=709" type="image/jpeg" height="709" width="1260"/></item><item><title><![CDATA[When is it time to stop? What a viral Hyrox moment says about pushing our bodies]]></title><link>https://www.thenationalnews.com/lifestyle/wellbeing/2026/09/18/hyrox-fitness-burnout-wellbeing-gym-injury/</link><guid isPermaLink="true">https://www.thenationalnews.com/lifestyle/wellbeing/2026/09/18/hyrox-fitness-burnout-wellbeing-gym-injury/</guid><dc:creator><![CDATA[Evelyn Lau]]></dc:creator><description></description><pubDate>Fri, 18 Sep 2026 18:01:00 +0000</pubDate><content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>A little ache here, some pain there – it’s a familiar feeling for <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/lifestyle/wellbeing/2025/12/24/fitness-trends-2026-community-workouts-walking-yoga-and-the-rise-of-recovery/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/lifestyle/wellbeing/2025/12/24/fitness-trends-2026-community-workouts-walking-yoga-and-the-rise-of-recovery/">fitness enthusiasts</a>. But when is it time to simply stop?</p><p>After an unfortunate incident at a recent <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/lifestyle/wellbeing/2025/07/16/hyrox-abu-dhabi-fitness-race/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/lifestyle/wellbeing/2025/07/16/hyrox-abu-dhabi-fitness-race/">Hyrox event</a> in Beijing in which a competitor soiled herself but continued competing – raising concerns over hygiene risks, shared equipment and athlete safety – the question is worth asking: at what point does perseverance stop being admirable and become a refusal to listen to what our bodies are telling us?</p><p>It is an extreme example and, for many of us, would seem like an obvious sign to stop. But on a less dramatic level, pushing our bodies beyond what they can comfortably handle is something many of us have probably done. You don't have to be an elite athlete to understand the mentality.</p><p>For most of this year, I had a nagging injury in my hand that caused pain through my wrist even during simple movements. A more sensible person would have gone to the doctor to figure out what was wrong. Instead, I decided to try to power through.</p><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/5J6YG44WPQCMI5KLP542JGO5ZY.jpg?auth=e8d3c495a6d903aed2d1f0592179837a7c4af16825f0773c2c7c188076f0d91d&smart=true&width=3708&height=2472" alt="Many people do not pay enough heed when their body signals something is wrong. Getty Images" height="2472" width="3708"/><p>Eventually, it became so painful that even something as simple as scratching my back was difficult, and I finally had it checked. I was diagnosed with De Quervain's tenosynovitis, a condition involving inflammation of the tendon sheath on the thumb side of the wrist.</p><p>Even then, I didn't really stop. I started <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/lifestyle/wellbeing/2022/09/28/when-and-why-to-see-a-chiropractor-physiotherapist-or-osteopath/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/lifestyle/wellbeing/2022/09/28/when-and-why-to-see-a-chiropractor-physiotherapist-or-osteopath/">physiotherapy</a> and somehow convinced myself that meant I could continue going to the <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/tags/fitness">gym</a>. After all, I had a routine that kept me going and one that – despite the pain – I enjoyed. Surely things would get better?</p><p>They didn't. After months and months of physio, I decided to have surgery to relieve the pain, finally accepting that perhaps I should have listened to my body sooner.</p><p>It's a strange balance. We are constantly told about the importance of discipline when it comes to exercise: get your steps in, stick to your routine and don't skip the gym simply because you don't feel like going. For the most part, that's useful advice.</p><p>But sometimes it can become difficult to distinguish between making excuses and having a legitimate reason to stop. Rest can feel like laziness, and taking time away from exercise can feel as though you're undoing months of progress. </p><p>In my case, I was so determined to not upend my routine that I failed to question whether maintaining it was doing me any good.</p><p>It isn't only in the gym where people try to persevere, either. At work, signs of burnout are often ignored, as if there's some weakness in listening to your body or brain when they signal you need a break.</p><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/IRFX3RZP6ZEIFDB4E2SMGKUBVQ.jpg?auth=9e2cbf156e643db185e5f8db4cf8bc892aa6d561d259a7232ab3b8bc179b1634&smart=true&width=4608&height=2756" alt="A 2025 study finds that 66 per cent of workers suffer from burnout. Victoria Heath / Unsplash" height="2756" width="4608"/><p>A 2025 report from Moodle found that 66 per cent of US workers have experienced some form of burnout. Brain fog, fatigue and muscle tension are among the common symptoms. Yet people tend to ignore these signs, believing what their bodies are telling them isn't serious enough to warrant taking a break.</p><p>Whether it's at work or in the gym, taking a break can feel like falling behind, particularly when everyone around us appears to be carrying on. We celebrate discipline, resilience and perseverance, but we don't talk enough about recognising when pushing through is no longer helping us.</p><p>There is, of course, value in pushing ourselves. Progress rarely comes from only doing what feels easy or comfortable, whether we're trying to run faster, lift heavier or simply get through a difficult day. </p><p>But knowing when to stop requires its own kind of discipline too. </p><p>My experience was nowhere near as extreme as what happened to Australian elite athlete Joanna Wietrzyk at Hyrox in Beijing, but the instinct behind it might feel more familiar than people would like to admit. My body spent months telling me something was wrong and, rather than listen, I kept finding ways to negotiate with it.</p><p>Sometimes pushing through is an achievement. Sometimes stopping is the smarter choice.</p>]]></content:encoded><media:content url="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/MR4NUORWAJEUFC5HXG6NBGLLSM.jpg?auth=8aa6157fbcbebdfc612695522595f65532a4ebec26ec1b586b1f294bc224dc68&amp;smart=true&amp;width=5301&amp;height=3534" type="image/jpeg" height="3534" width="5301"><media:description type="plain"><![CDATA[Pushing themselves beyond what they can comfortably handle is something many fitness enthusiasts and professional go-getters have likely done. Mathieu Improvisato / Unsplash]]></media:description><media:credit role="author" scheme="urn:ebu">Mathieu Improvisato</media:credit></media:content></item><item><title><![CDATA[Sing to your own tune: Fafa on why UAE artists need to back their original music]]></title><link>https://www.thenationalnews.com/arts-culture/music-stage/2026/09/18/fafa-fatima-emirati-honduran-singer-hidden-bar-abu-dhabi/</link><guid isPermaLink="true">https://www.thenationalnews.com/arts-culture/music-stage/2026/09/18/fafa-fatima-emirati-honduran-singer-hidden-bar-abu-dhabi/</guid><dc:creator><![CDATA[Saeed Saeed]]></dc:creator><description></description><pubDate>Fri, 18 Sep 2026 18:01:00 +0000</pubDate><content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>When Fafa performed the first of two planned shows at Abu Dhabi’s Hidden Bar in April, she faced the usual questions asked of a local musician: was there enough space, enough time and, most importantly, would people show up?</p><p>The gig, featuring Fafa and her band playing original material spanning neo-soul, jazz and R&amp;B, left her with more welcome problems. The buzz around the show led to a follow-up a month later, which sold out, with people gathering outside the venue to listen to snatches of the performance.</p><p>The show was also recorded and released as <i>The Veiled Essence Experience: Live at The Hidden Bar</i>, a new live album capturing the Emirati-Honduran singer in a setting she had long wanted to see more of in the UAE.</p><p>The performances were part of the Music in the City initiative by Department of Culture and Tourism Abu Dhabi, in part to display some of the the breadth of the city’s music venues.</p><p>The turnout also suggested there was public appetite to hear original material from the local music scene.</p><p>“It just serves as a reminder that we have we have creativity and we have purpose,” Fafa, whose real name is Fatima bin Safwan, tells <i>The National</i>.</p><p>“We are so used to importing talent from abroad that it became normal. And so I want to normalise the idea that creativity already exists here.”</p><p>It is a conversation likely to become more prominent across the local music industry, from venues and promoters to singers and songwriters, when <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/uae/2026/08/14/uaes-music-royalty-fee-will-benefit-creative-community-say-local-business-owners/" target="_blank" rel="" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/uae/2026/08/14/uaes-music-royalty-fee-will-benefit-creative-community-say-local-business-owners/">new royalty rules</a> come into effect on December 1.</p><blockquote class="instagram-media" data-instgrm-captioned data-instgrm-permalink="https://www.instagram.com/reel/DbqoGE8IH0Q/?utm_source=ig_embed&amp;utm_campaign=loading" style="background-color: #F4F4F4; border-radius: 50%; height: 12.5px; width: 12.5px; transform: translateX(0px) translateY(7px);"></div> <div style="background-color: #F4F4F4; height: 12.5px; transform: rotate(-45deg) translateX(3px) translateY(1px); width: 12.5px; flex-grow: 0; margin-right: 14px; margin-left: 2px;"></div> <div style="background-color: #F4F4F4; border-radius: 50%; height: 12.5px; width: 12.5px; transform: translateX(9px) translateY(-18px);"></div></div><div style="margin-left: 8px;"> <div style=" background-color: #F4F4F4; border-radius: 50%; flex-grow: 0; height: 20px; width: 20px;"></div> <div style=" width: 0; height: 0; border-top: 2px solid transparent; border-left: 6px solid #f4f4f4; border-bottom: 2px solid transparent; transform: translateX(16px) translateY(-4px) rotate(30deg)"></div></div><div style="margin-left: auto;"> <div style=" width: 0px; border-top: 8px solid #F4F4F4; border-right: 8px solid transparent; transform: translateY(16px);"></div> <div style=" background-color: #F4F4F4; flex-grow: 0; height: 12px; width: 16px; transform: translateY(-4px);"></div> <div style=" width: 0; height: 0; border-top: 8px solid #F4F4F4; border-left: 8px solid transparent; transform: translateY(-4px) translateX(8px);"></div></div></div> <div style="display: flex; flex-direction: column; flex-grow: 1; justify-content: center; margin-bottom: 24px;"> <div style=" background-color: #F4F4F4; border-radius: 4px; flex-grow: 0; height: 14px; margin-bottom: 6px; width: 224px;"></div> <div style=" background-color: #F4F4F4; border-radius: 4px; flex-grow: 0; height: 14px; width: 144px;"></div></div></a></div></blockquote><p>Premises including restaurants, cafes, hotels, malls and fitness centres will be required to obtain licences for copyrighted music. Under the system announced by the Ministry of Economy, fees will vary according to the size of the business and how the music is used, with live music and DJ performances attracting higher rates.</p><p>The system is designed to create a more formal route for songwriters and rights holders to receive royalties when their music is used commercially.</p><p>“It’s 100 per cent a game changer because now when I perform, I’m not only going to get paid for the performance, I’m also getting paid to perform my songs because of copyright,” Fafa says. “So now I’m getting two sources of income.”</p><p>But she says the commercial initiative should not be the reason an artist turns to original material.</p><p>“If your drive is to make money, then you need to get out of the game. If your driving factor is, ‘I’m going to do my originals now because I’m going to make money,’ you’re in it for the wrong reasons,” she says.</p><p>“The change can still encourage artists by giving original music another source of income, but what it really does is show that the wider system is beginning to recognise the value of artists creating and performing their own work.”</p><p>Fafa has been performing in the UAE for nearly a decade, with a sound that draws on R&amp;B, soul, blues and jazz. She has performed at events including Bred Abu Dhabi and Sikka Art Festival in Dubai, as well as <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/arts-culture/music/2022/08/28/from-ahlam-to-balqees-five-emirati-women-winning-music-fans-by-singing-with-purpose/" target="_blank" rel="" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/arts-culture/music/2022/08/28/from-ahlam-to-balqees-five-emirati-women-winning-music-fans-by-singing-with-purpose/">supporting Bruno Mars</a> in Abu Dhabi. </p><iframe style="border-radius: 12px" width="100%" height="352" title="Spotify Embed: The Veiled Essence Experience: Live at The Hidden Bar" frameborder="0" allowfullscreen allow="autoplay; clipboard-write; encrypted-media; fullscreen; picture-in-picture" loading="lazy" src="https://open.spotify.com/embed/album/6mHiJaca8L8MwH09dD8Qu9?utm_source=oembed"></iframe><p>Fafa is now recording studio versions of songs featured on the live album and plans to release physical editions incorporating art and poetry.</p><p>Once that material is released, she plans to return to UAE stages and hopes the conversation around those performances has evolved between venue and promoters and performers. Fafa adds that artists also need to be more assertive about playing their own work.</p><p>“Venues will tell you to play Top 40. It’s up to you whether you want to listen to that venue or not, because last time I checked, the venue is not an entertainment manager. Last time I checked, they’re not a musical director. Last time I checked, they hired you to do the entertainment,” she says.</p><p>“You as the entertainer should know better. And it gets to a point where they start hiring you because of that. But you have a proof of concept and you can bring in your own fan base. And if they don’t hire you back, well, it gives you a chance to learn and rework it.”</p><p>Before the Hidden Bar shows, Fafa had also begun to question why she should leave the UAE to chase opportunities when musicians from abroad were coming here to find them.</p><p>Her time studying songwriting and production in New York and Los Angeles had shown her what repeated access to open mics and small stages could do for an artist.</p><p>“Our experiences here as artists in the UAE and really the region are much different than in the States because we’re building the infrastructure ourselves,” Fafa says.</p><blockquote class="instagram-media" data-instgrm-captioned data-instgrm-permalink="https://www.instagram.com/reel/DcO_klENNax/?utm_source=ig_embed&amp;utm_campaign=loading"
