href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/arts-culture/music/2021/11/10/what-a-unesco-city-of-music-status-for-abu-dhabi-means-for-the-cultural-sector/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/arts-culture/music/2021/11/10/what-a-unesco-city-of-music-status-for-abu-dhabi-means-for-the-cultural-sector/">Unesco Creative City of Music</a> and the incoming royalty system among signs of change.</p><p>“The government and the policymakers are already giving the tools and the resources to create that structure,” she says. “If there’s something happening that they’re unaware of, they are more than receptive to that information.”</p><p>Fafa is equally clear that those structures do not remove responsibility from artists.</p><p>“We are the ones who create the art. They give us the tools and enable the platform for us to be on there. If there’s something missing, then that is a conversation to be had,” she says.</p><p>“It is a relationship between the policymakers, government and the artists. We all need to be in the room. However, sometimes we do have to take it into our own hands, in a way that is still respectful and within the tools that are available.”</p>]]></content:encoded><media:content url="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/6Y7YALJRE5B37KGOJZ6CJXWBPE.jpeg?auth=251a6a55e84bdeb003516ff1051f7bf2dff62245d0ec258974622d20dc39f0b5&amp;smart=true&amp;width=4338&amp;height=2892" type="image/jpeg" height="2892" width="4338"><media:description type="plain"><![CDATA[The singer says UAE artists need to perform more original music and build audiences for their own work. Photo: Fafa]]></media:description></media:content></item><item><title><![CDATA[Address Downtown review: Rest, recreation and Dubai Mall on your doorstep]]></title><link>https://www.thenationalnews.com/travel/2026/09/18/address-downtown-dubai-mall-review/</link><guid isPermaLink="true">https://www.thenationalnews.com/travel/2026/09/18/address-downtown-dubai-mall-review/</guid><dc:creator><![CDATA[Panna Munyal]]></dc:creator><description></description><pubDate>Fri, 18 Sep 2026 18:01:00 +0000</pubDate><content:encoded><![CDATA[<p><div style="float:left;padding-top:5px;padding-right:10px"><a href=https://www.thenationalnews.com/tags/hotel-insider/ target="_blank"><img alt="Hotel Insider" src=https://thenational-the-national-prod.cdn.arcpublishing.com/resizer/v2/IXFQCXQW5ZCCXNFQTMNOXYOOKI.jpg?smart=true&auth=737ed11b01bb89b6ba097a05629bee3d72fcb5671e604148b317e6d7dbd25c7c width="120" height="120" loading="lazy"/></a></div></p><p>A worthy staycation tends to factor in a healthy dose of R&amp;R. Staying in your home city or country often takes away the pressure – as well as the pleasure – of cramming in as many sights as your stamina will allow, signing up for as many activities as your savings can handle and eating at as many restaurants as your stomach can accommodate.</p><p>And then I visited Address Downtown.</p><h2><b>The welcome</b></h2><p>The invigorating scent of vetiver and cedar <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/lifestyle/2021/10/21/does-scent-keep-you-in-the-mall-for-longer-the-link-between-aroma-and-retail-therapy/" target="_blank" rel="" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/lifestyle/2021/10/21/does-scent-keep-you-in-the-mall-for-longer-the-link-between-aroma-and-retail-therapy/">dominates my first impression</a> of the property. That, and the equally heady blend of having <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/tags/burj-khalifa">Burj Khalifa</a> and Dubai Fountain within view. </p><p>The pearlescent counters of the lobby are offset by colourful glass displays of the latest fashion from Elisabetta Franchi, Elie Saab and Manish Malhotra, among others. My family of three enjoy complimentary dates and <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/arts-culture/2025/05/12/coffee-ceremony-arabic-gulf-gahwa-brewing-tradition/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/arts-culture/2025/05/12/coffee-ceremony-arabic-gulf-gahwa-brewing-tradition/">gahwa</a> while check-in formalities are completed, and wander around the buzzing and deceptively large reception area. </p><p>The area is broken up with nooks filled with the aforementioned window displays, plus plush seating, an excellent cafe and pastry counter, glimmering chandeliers, water installations, larger-than-life sculptures (the iridescent butterflies enthral my six-year-old) and even a grocery shop offering fresh coconut water. As such, our luggage precedes us to the room by nearly an hour. </p><h2><b>The room</b></h2><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/CAXFSNAP35CRNMD7CGTX4ZT6XM.JPG?auth=25b8236d797f424271d66a3d15ab704d8961bc89987f713df349b6943d5dca4b&smart=true&width=8256&height=5504" alt="Burj Khalifa and Dubai Fountain views come alive after sunset. Photo: Emaar" height="5504" width="8256"/><p>We stay in a 70-square-metre, one-bedroom Residence suite facing Dubai Fountain and Burj Khalifa. This has a separate bedroom and a living room with a sofa bed, plus a kitchen stocked with all the white goods, and a study with fitted floor-to-ceiling shelves on three sides. The latter is ideal for those on a working holiday, or those with children who insist on carting their stuffed toys around – or, indeed, those who need the extra room for their <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/tags/dubai-mall">Dubai Mall</a> shopping bags. </p><p>Given the space dedicated to the kitchen and study, the suite feels quite compact, with a queen bed and a shared bathroom that has a shower, rather than a tub or both – a la UAE five-star hotels. </p><p>But the space is enough for smaller families, and any quibbles I have are put to rest once I step on to the expansive balcony. The world’s tallest building feels close enough to touch, even when the world's <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/lifestyle/2025/09/26/dubai-fountain-opening-october-fun-facts/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/lifestyle/2025/09/26/dubai-fountain-opening-october-fun-facts/">largest choreographed fountain</a> dances directly in our line of sight every 30 minutes. No matter that I’ve seen it several times from a distance, the ever-changing illuminated facade of Burj Khalifa is awe-inspiring at this proximity. </p><h2><b>The neighbourhood</b></h2><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/UU7OVAWAXNEKPEZWZ3ET3X62DU.jpg?auth=fbc8b881be7d8603958d0a5e75e080c5812c2d3e0c34a2f4220c00ebfd5770f1&smart=true&width=5712&height=4284" alt="Dubai Mall is a three-minute walk from the hotel with a dedicated pedestrian bridge. Chris Whiteoak / The National" height="4284" width="5712"/><p>The time it takes to reach Dubai Mall, find parking there and eventually drag yourself away has become something of a running joke among my circle of friends. My husband and I have avoided it over the past 15 years unless we can help it. To have a pedestrian bridge that gets us to the Human Waterfall section from our room in three minutes is a revelation – primarily of my inner shopaholic.</p><p>One of the largest in the world by area, the mall stocks collections from several labels and brands not found elsewhere in the UAE. So my alter ego decided to go on a mission to browse and buy as many one-offs as 24 leisurely hours would allow, returning after each meal and once again the next day. Being able to head to and from the room as often as we liked was a game-changer. </p><p>We even had enough time to check out the excellent Dubai Aquarium and Underwater Zoo, and talk about catching a show at <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/arts-culture/music-stage/2026/09/10/dubai-opera-10-year-anniversary-full-show-line-up-2026-2027/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/arts-culture/music-stage/2026/09/10/dubai-opera-10-year-anniversary-full-show-line-up-2026-2027/">Dubai Opera</a> when we return sans child, all on foot of course. </p><h2><b>The food</b></h2><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/MT7O5UYBBBARFMEM7RXHY2ISNI.jpg?auth=d35b6a41ae60e4253546ddfe5fdcf409b2a27e137b542d401d6b89e83676b15a&smart=true&width=6120&height=4480" alt="Beef carpaccio with black truffle and mustard is a standout dish at Cetara. Photo: Address Downtown" height="4480" width="6120"/><p>All-day restaurant Italian Cetara serves a great family brunch every weekend, a steal at Dh145. It offers premium dishes such as beef carpaccio with black truffle shavings and piquant mustard; pan-seared sea bass with sweet potato puree; and profiteroles that tick both the crunchy and creamy columns. </p><p>We also sampled the Italian restaurant’s a la carte menu, highlights of which include hamachi ceviche with caviar and lemon emulsion (Dh95); baked eggplant with aged Parmesan and basil sauce (Dh80); and baby chicken with rosemary jus (Dh145), plus a vanilla panna cotta (Dh50) veritably buried under a garnish of fresh berries, for dessert. This is simple yet confident cooking was served with a sunny smile.</p><p>The space itself mirrors the service. Its airy, open-plan layout with floor-to-ceiling windows looking on to a Burj-facing terrace make it a must-reserve spot come winter. </p><p>Guests can also ask the concierge to book a spot at a sister property, so one evening we dined at Solara at Address Dubai Mall. Particularly well-executed dishes from its Arabic-Mediterranean menu include: creamy tomato prawns with garlic croutons (Dh125); Australian lamb chops with lemony couscous (Dh195); and the classic truffle pasta with porcini puree (Dh155). </p><p>An amuse-bouche of a hummus-muhammara hybrid lathered with pesto surpassed the hazelnut hummus (Dh45), while the caramelised butternut squash with harissa oil (Dh45) was a bit too sweet and cold for me.</p><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/HPM4NWBAHFBFDDERTT7VTBVN5U.jpg?auth=2132dc5271f54535d8ce0c7655ca30315337f19b053b1532e4abc2aeeebcd6d2&smart=true&width=4739&height=3385" alt="The profiteroles are a must-try at Cetara. Photo: Address Downtown" height="3385" width="4739"/><p>Cetara serves a buffet as well as a tableside menu until 11am each morning. From the latter, my usual go-to of truffled-poached eggs was excellent, yet it paled in comparison to my first taste of Turkish cilbir eggs, with its mix of Greek yoghurt, toasted garlic, dried mint and burnt paprika butter elevating the dish. The menu also takes in avo-toast, acai bowls, English porridge and granola-fruit parfait. </p><p>The breakfast buffet, meanwhile, is a mix of international, Arabic and Asian cuisines – with everything from American and the full English staples, to idli, dosa, aloo paratha and chana masala from the Indian subcontinent, and freshly made manakeesh and foul medames. </p><p>A whole room doubles as the pastry corner, serving all manner of sweet and savoury breads; half a dozen types of honey and jam from home-grown brand Seedra; and even a generous gluten-free selection of individually wrapped marble, walnut and almond cakes and vegan oat protein balls. </p><p>There’s enough here for my family of three to not repeat a single dish over the two mornings of our stay. </p><h2><b>Insider tip</b></h2><p>While I didn’t leave the mall long enough to visit The Spa Longevity, a glance through its menu revealed treatments that go well beyond typical facials and massages. </p><p>Thermal dry floating with alternating heat and cold exposure (from Dh150), non-invasive Endospheres face therapy with micro-vibration technology (Dh630) and Light Therapy with cold atmospheric plasma (Dh350) are all worthy contenders for those prioritising rest and recuperation during their stay at Address Downtown. </p><h2><b>The verdict</b></h2><p>The hotel is a microcosm of the multifaceted Dubai life, offering everything from cultural and family activities to excellent dining and spa facilities, and – given its proximity to Dubai Mall – the chance to shop but not drop. </p><h2><b>Bottom line</b> </h2><p>Rates at Address Downtown start from Dh1,500 per night, depending on room category and season. Check-in is from 2pm, checkout is at noon.</p><p><i>This review was conducted at the invitation of the hotel and reflects hotel standards during this time</i></p>]]></content:encoded><media:content url="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/HVKXJPN76JGSDEU3IBSQD53H44.JPG?auth=18aeb951cc0d715567f50eefe55bc73c4f7794fc9e521a928b52abd51b29572a&amp;smart=true&amp;width=8256&amp;height=5504" type="image/jpeg" height="5504" width="8256"><media:description type="plain"><![CDATA[A spa suite with fountain views is one of several room categories. Photo: Emaar]]></media:description><media:credit role="author" scheme="urn:ebu">Nicolas Dumont</media:credit></media:content></item><item><title><![CDATA[Anker Soundcore Liberty 5 Pro Max review: These AI-powered earbuds are listening]]></title><link>https://www.thenationalnews.com/future/technology/2026/09/18/anker-soundcore-liberty-5-pro-max-review-ai-recorder/</link><guid isPermaLink="true">https://www.thenationalnews.com/future/technology/2026/09/18/anker-soundcore-liberty-5-pro-max-review-ai-recorder/</guid><dc:creator><![CDATA[Alvin R Cabral]]></dc:creator><description></description><pubDate>Fri, 18 Sep 2026 18:01:00 +0000</pubDate><content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>In a consumer electronics market flooded with choice, standing out matters. One way is to look noticeably different; another is to <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/future/technology/2026/09/10/apples-foldable-phone-launched-everything-you-need-to-know-about-the-dh8499-iphone-duo/" target="_blank" rel="">sound like something familiar</a>. With its Soundcore Liberty 5 Pro Max wireless headphones, Anker has managed to do both.</p><p>These handy sound cans come with a case featuring a touchscreen display on top. That in itself isn't new – rivals such as JBL and Honor have done it before – but Anker's version has a few tricks up its sleeve.</p><p>Then there's the name. Liberty 5 Pro Max has a distinctly Apple-esque ring to it, while its finish brings to mind the <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/future/technology/2025/09/30/apple-iphone-17-pro-max-along-with-the-iphone-air-its-the-best-upgrade-cycle/" target="_blank" rel="">Cosmic Orange</a> – and now <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/future/technology/2026/09/10/apples-foldable-phone-launched-everything-you-need-to-know-about-the-dh8499-iphone-duo/" target="_blank" rel="">burgundy</a> – devices that have been doing the rounds on social media.</p><p>Oh, and these earbuds also hold a Guinness World Record for the “highest speech quality score for TWS [true wireless stereo] earbuds”. Take that, competition.</p><h2><b>Sound and feel</b></h2><p>The earbuds are comfortable, and out of the box you get five ear-tip sizes, ranging from XXS to large, so there should be a fit for most ears. Not once did they slip out of ours – except during a vertigo-inducing headbang test that probably shouldn't be a daily occurrence.</p><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/XXJLZ27QUNEXZE2BGGPXK3WHH4.jpg?auth=93dbd93e077bc80a9ee85652d2de6b90b2a61813ad09cb48e85347e2423f165c&smart=true&width=1600&height=2000" alt="Anker's Soundcore Liberty 5 Pro Max has a minimalistic design and is easy to stow away. Photo: Anker" height="2000" width="1600"/><p>Their curvy <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/tags/design">design</a> also helps them stand out from rivals that can resemble tiny scrubbers with handles dangling from your ears. The result is a more minimalist and stylish aesthetic. There are two colours, titanium gold and midnight black, and we're all for the metallic beauty of the former.</p><p>The 1.78-inch (4.52cm) Amoled display is a nice touch – pun intended – and operates much like a smartwatch. Swipe left or right to reveal options including media playback, equaliser presets and ambient sound modes. Swiping down brings up the control centre – sounds familiar? – while swiping up reveals the device's biggest selling point.</p><p>Battery life, however, is a bit of a downer. Anker rates the earbuds at 6.5 hours with active noise cancellation switched on, with another 22 hours available from the charging case. For comparison, <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/business/technology/2021/10/28/review-apples-new-airpods-find-middle-ground-between-high-end-and-entry-level-segments/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/business/technology/2021/10/28/review-apples-new-airpods-find-middle-ground-between-high-end-and-entry-level-segments/">Apple's AirPods Pro 3</a> last up to eight hours with ANC enabled, with an additional 24 hours from their case.</p><h2><b>May AI help you</b></h2><p>That last action brings up, among other features, note-taking, powered by <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/tags/artificial-intelligence">artificial intelligence</a> (of course).</p><p>You do this with the charging case, and your recordings get synced with the Soundcore app that in turn will generate transcripts, in addition to translation supporting more than 100 languages. We tested it out in four situations: a quiet room, a room with some people but with the speaker from a distance, a mall food court and a conference in a huge hall where the audio from a panel blurted out from speakers.</p><p>The first three resulted in accurate transcripts, while the last – the conference situation – still pretty much nailed it. I would attribute any inaccuracy to the mics being used and the audio breaking off at times, but we pretty much got what we wanted.</p><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/Y7PCNWQSLBCWLCNT2R4GMDXRDY.jpg?auth=b7d4c2682bc927f55ae2de0cee0a7c93ef29d226daf1e323173d5c2a89576163&smart=true&width=1600&height=2000" alt="The AI-powered recorder will generate transcripts in addition to translation supporting more than 100 languages. Photo: Anker" height="2000" width="1600"/><p>This tool is very useful. Normally, I'd have two phones, with one acting as a recorder – having a recorder this small, powered by AI, is a good deal. Something to note: the device cannot capture audio from phone calls, online meetings or video calls.</p><p>However – and it's a fairly big catch – to fully maximise its capabilities, Anker is offering a subscription. By default, the device includes a free starter plan that gives you 120 minutes of AI transcription a month for two years. Signing up for pro gives you 1,200 minutes monthly for Dh59 ($16) a month, Dh257 for six months and Dh367 annually, while going for unlimited will set you back Dh880 per year.<b> </b>Extra minutes can also be bought.</p><p>That sounds like services offered by platforms such as Otter but the limitation you have with your free minutes might come back to bite you sooner or later. It isn't fun scrambling for minutes when you need them, trust me. I'm not really sure if the subscription model would turn off some users, but I also see the revenue angle here. Fair game.</p><h2><b>Verdict</b></h2><p>At Dh899 – the base Pro option comes at Dh699 – the Anker Soundcore Liberty 5 Pro Max is still a good deal, given its utility for those who really need it. I just wish it had more free minutes or lower subscription prices.</p><p>It's also stylish and minimalistic, with no over-the-top mess especially on the display. It's not the most affordable of the lot, but it deserves a good look.</p>]]></content:encoded><media:content url="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/C27IQHGEJBG2TLJQFZMROPVSXE.png?auth=cdf92aa8ef8dbaabfb20d714b73c5da7042b20b9a71771228c27fc406c6fedbb&amp;smart=true&amp;width=1600&amp;height=1032" type="image/png" height="1032" width="1600"><media:description type="plain"><![CDATA[Anker's Soundcore Liberty 5 Pro Max comes with 1,200 free transcription minutes – after that you can either buy more or sign up to a subscription plan. Photo: Anker]]></media:description></media:content></item><item><title><![CDATA[Have archaeologists confirmed where Jesus was buried? Remarkable findings from Christianity’s earliest days ]]></title><link>https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/mena/2026/09/18/where-jesus-buried-burial-spot-christianity-jerusalem/</link><guid isPermaLink="true">https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/mena/2026/09/18/where-jesus-buried-burial-spot-christianity-jerusalem/</guid><dc:creator><![CDATA[Thomas Helm]]></dc:creator><description></description><pubDate>Fri, 18 Sep 2026 18:00:00 +0000</pubDate><content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>Centuries of war, fire, earthquakes and renovations have left Jerusalem’s <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/mena/2026/03/29/israel-criticised-after-police-prevent-cardinal-from-celebrating-centuries-old-christian-devotions/" target="_blank" rel="" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/mena/2026/03/29/israel-criticised-after-police-prevent-cardinal-from-celebrating-centuries-old-christian-devotions/">Church of the Holy Sepulchre</a> in a spectacular but chaotic state, a hodgepodge of styles reflecting the many communities that stake a claim to the site. </p><p>In recent years, major work has taken place to restore and excavate parts of the building. That followed a sudden <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/mena/2026/09/17/israel-triples-e1-construction-plan-with-large-tender-despite-international-condemnation/" target="_blank" rel="" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/mena/2026/09/17/israel-triples-e1-construction-plan-with-large-tender-despite-international-condemnation/">Israeli</a> assessment a decade ago that found the church’s most important structure – the chamber known as the Edicule, said to house the burial site of Jesus – was in a dangerous state.</p><p>For a few hours, the chamber was emptied. The Greek Patriarch protested against at the lack of warning. </p><p>The restoration work has, however, resulted in remarkable findings about early Christianity. </p><p>The Bible tells Christians where Jesus was buried and, they believe, resurrected. The Gospel of John says the tomb was in a "garden", an account that may have received scientific support after Italian archaeologists presented botanical and pollen evidence found during a dig. The remains of grape vines and olive trees were found in a floor section next to the Edicule. </p><p>“As archaeologists, we can say that this clear proof of agriculture near the venerated tomb and in the area of Golgotha during the time of Jesus makes the area compatible with the Gospel narrative, especially John,” Father Amedeo Ricco, one of the archaeologists who excavated the church, told <i>The National</i>. </p><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/DOCEVAPRVZ5THVOYR3GMAAEG2U.jpg?auth=51ecfcaf7e13409a27c380b95e41f3abc7e725412112519fa2ff0eaf44eff709&smart=true&width=5472&height=3648" alt="Catholic clergymen hold prayers next to the Edicule, the chamber said to house the tomb of Jesus. EPA " height="3648" width="5472"/><p>More findings could now be on the way. Last week, church leaders involved in the project gathered in <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/mena/2026/09/09/israel-gives-british-consulate-in-jerusalem-30-days-to-close/" target="_blank" rel="" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/mena/2026/09/09/israel-gives-british-consulate-in-jerusalem-30-days-to-close/">Jerusalem</a> for an update on the research and restoration phase, now that the archaeological digs have concluded.</p><p>A first volume on the project, containing more than 500 pages of articles, maps and images, was shown to representatives of the churches involved. More volumes of the Hagia Polis Project are expected as researchers comb through their results and scholars review the findings.</p><p>“It’s the biggest and most invasive project that took place in the church since the end of the Ottoman period,” said Father Athanasius Macora, secretary of the Status Quo for the Custody of the Holy Land, an order of Franciscan friars that looks after Christian sites in the area.</p><p>The Status Quo is a pact dating back to 1852 that governs the often tense management of the Holy Sepulchre, where different denominations and communities have a history of quarrelling, even over seemingly minor details.</p><p>Like the church that houses it, the area that is believed to be the site of Jesus’s burial has taken many forms and seen much drama. For almost two centuries, it was covered by pagan temples, an attempt by the Roman emperor Hadrian to erase the memory of the site. Other setbacks included a fire lit by Persian troops in 614, the complete destruction of the church in 1009 by the Fatimid caliph Al Hakim, and another major fire in 1809. </p><p>The decision to go ahead with major renovations was made in 2019, after repairs were made to the Edicule two years earlier. Previous attempts at restoration were often compromised by infighting. </p><p>“The archaeology was very slow because of Covid-19 and the Gaza war. There was a lot of stopping and starting. There was an advantage at least in that there weren’t any pilgrims, so it was easy for the crew to work when they could work,” Father Athanasius added. “There were also cases where communities inside the church had to cancel their liturgies, but we tried to keep a regular prayer service going.”</p><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/FESAP5TMHJCU5AJWDZBWREOEXU.jpg?auth=e4bac9d48e32eef8d0ac72f24b4206af33d000de467c3ad3a78619f1887befe9&smart=true&width=5018&height=3345" alt="The Greek Orthodox Patriarch of Jerusalem, Theophilos III, at the Church of the Holy Sepulchre in 2023. Willy Lowry / The National" height="3345" width="5018"/><p>In a place where fights have broken out over priests moving chairs and sweeping contested areas, it was a major achievement to agree on a project that interrupted the habits of Christian communities in such a profound way. In normal times, the church is the site of a constant stream of services and liturgies, day and night, which require continuing, and sometimes fraught, management. </p><p>The results seen today from the decision to co-operate are now being hailed as an example of what can be achieved when communities choose to work together.</p><p>Father Francesco Patton, the most senior member of the Custody of the Holy Land, said the co-operation between the three major communities in the Holy Sepulchre – Catholics, Greek Orthodox and Armenian Orthodox – “holds not merely archaeological, historical, or artistic value, but above all ecclesial and ecumenical significance”. </p><p>“It demonstrates that Jerusalem’s Christians can collaborate and that the Churches of Jerusalem – through centuries of coexistence at the Holy Sepulchre and ceaseless daily worship – are far closer and more united than the world might assume.”</p>]]></content:encoded><media:content url="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/OZJWWNR7YVETBAMZEJPKGPQOB4.jpg?auth=4cca9e0add1e41060039457660709f8556a06f72bc42564b82e167faf6a8079c&amp;smart=true&amp;width=6144&amp;height=4096" type="image/jpeg" height="4096" width="6144"><media:description type="plain"><![CDATA[Christians believe Jesus was buried in what is now the Church of the Holy Sepulchre in Jerusalem. Getty Images ]]></media:description><media:credit role="author" scheme="urn:ebu">Lior Mizrahi</media:credit></media:content></item><item><title><![CDATA[A millennium of fear and fascination: How the Arab world has shaped Paris]]></title><link>https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/2026/09/18/arab-influence-paris-france/</link><guid isPermaLink="true">https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/2026/09/18/arab-influence-paris-france/</guid><dc:creator><![CDATA[Sunniva Rose]]></dc:creator><description></description><pubDate>Fri, 18 Sep 2026 18:00:00 +0000</pubDate><content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>Imagine walking through the narrow streets of the Latin Quarter <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/europe/2026/09/09/fourteen-suspected-migrant-smugglers-go-on-trial-in-paris-over-mass-drowning-in-channel/" target="_blank" rel="" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/europe/2026/09/09/fourteen-suspected-migrant-smugglers-go-on-trial-in-paris-over-mass-drowning-in-channel/">in Paris</a>, with its cobblestones, cafes and bookshops. Now imagine seeing those streets for the first time as an Egyptian intellectual in the early 19th century. </p><p>Rifaa Al Tahtawi's observations might sound surprisingly familiar today.</p><p>The French, he wrote, were a curious people with a fascination <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/arts-culture/art-design/2026/07/27/renoir-masterpiece-now-on-display-at-louvre-abu-dhabi/" target="_blank" rel="" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/arts-culture/art-design/2026/07/27/renoir-masterpiece-now-on-display-at-louvre-abu-dhabi/">for museums</a>. They were fond of money but also of state intervention. They placed the poor and the old in dedicated institutions. Women, he observed, ruled the household. </p><p>Those were some of the thoughts penned to paper by Al Tahtawi in 1834 in his critical yet forgiving book, <i>An Imam in Paris,</i> after spending five years in the French capital as imam to the first Egyptian educational mission to France. It became one of the most influential early accounts by an Arab intellectual of European society. </p><p>Today, few French people have heard of him. Part of the reason may be that France’s relationship with the Arab world is often discussed through the prism of <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/europe/2024/09/09/gaza-war-exacerbates-hidden-identity-politics-in-france/" target="_blank" rel="" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/europe/2024/09/09/gaza-war-exacerbates-hidden-identity-politics-in-france/">contemporary politics</a>, leaving less space for the longer and more complicated history of cultural and intellectual exchange.</p><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/QQTAGJW6KBDZ3HSTJWDNDPWH2U.png?auth=d54627769482648b71ddf201434c695f11bea9bb2b53c11f773e82a2a5407762&smart=true&width=758&height=877" alt="The Egyptian intellectual Rifaa Al Tahtawi wrote a book about his experiences in 19th-century Paris" height="877" width="758"/><p>One of the bodies seeking to fill that gap is the <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/europe/2026/02/09/frances-arab-world-institute-seeks-new-president-after-epstein-connection-pushes-jack-lang-out/" target="_blank" rel="" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/europe/2026/02/09/frances-arab-world-institute-seeks-new-president-after-epstein-connection-pushes-jack-lang-out/">Arab World Institute</a>, a vibrant institution that hosts exhibitions, concerts, Arabic language classes and the largest collection of modern and contemporary Arab art in the Western world. </p><p><i>The National</i> recently joined one of its popular summer tours. With anti-migration and anti-Islam rhetoric on the rise, the chance to learn about how bridges have been built, and sometimes burnt, has rarely felt more timely. The tour offered a reminder that <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/arts-culture/2026/09/11/anne-claire-legendre-wants-regions-artists-to-have-greater-say-at-pariss-arab-world-institute/" target="_blank" rel="" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/arts-culture/2026/09/11/anne-claire-legendre-wants-regions-artists-to-have-greater-say-at-pariss-arab-world-institute/">Arab-French relations</a> go back at least a millennium and cannot easily be reduced to the politics of the present.</p><p>“The concept was to revisit the relationship between France and the Arab world – a relationship shaped by fear and fascination, depending on the era, and often by both,” Marie-Georges Nida, speaker and art historian at the Arab World Institute, told visitors as they started the tour.</p><h2><b>First impressions</b></h2><p>Being made up of people who had paid to spend several hours of a rainy Saturday afternoon walking through Paris learning about Arab-French history, the audience was in general more knowledgeable than average about the Middle East.</p><blockquote><p>There are things, you realise, that haven’t really changed all that much</p><p class="citation">Marie-Georges Nida, guide at the Arab World Institute</p></blockquote><p>Yet many were surprised to hear that France’s relationship with the Arab world dates back centuries, predating the colonial expansion of the late 19th century. Some were also unaware of France’s historic ties with Arab Christian communities in the Levant, which have contributed to the enduring importance of Lebanon and Syria <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/europe/2026/09/16/aoun-and-king-abdullah-to-meet-in-paris-as-france-leads-unifil-replacement-drive/" target="_blank" rel="" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/europe/2026/09/16/aoun-and-king-abdullah-to-meet-in-paris-as-france-leads-unifil-replacement-drive/">in French diplomacy</a>.</p><p>But what generated the most curiosity were Al Tahtawi’s observations of Paris and its people through the eyes of a foreign visitor. His journey came in the wake of <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/opinion/comment/2025/09/12/napoleon-trade-war-lessons/" target="_blank" rel="" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/opinion/comment/2025/09/12/napoleon-trade-war-lessons/">Emperor Napoleon Bonaparte’s</a> 1798 invasion of Egypt, a fiasco for France that nevertheless opened the door to cultural exchanges between the two countries.</p><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/AF4AQZSZQZDLXN24JTVMVVZPMA.jpg?auth=cde76cf330eb6c8a31de93035e6902ee64deb823b5fb641e1c985b529c0c758e&smart=true&width=5616&height=3737" alt="The Arab World Institute. AFP" height="3737" width="5616"/><p>“He was the darling of the Parisian elite and the Parisian bourgeoisie; everyone was clamouring for him. He was welcomed into people's homes and frequented the opera, theatres and racetracks, discovering the full scope of Parisian social life,” Ms Nida said. “He was incredibly curious.”</p><p>In his book, he spoke of the contrast between Cairo, which had been little developed by the Ottomans, and Paris, with its paved streets and street lights. He admired the French for their appetite for knowledge and numerous museums. But he was also critical of what he saw as a failure to care adequately for those in need, Ms Nida said.</p><p>“He said people are sordidly avaricious – that’s the term he used, 'sordid avarice' – and relied on the state to make up for that sordid avarice. He talked about the race for money. There are things, you realise, that haven’t really changed all that much,” she said.</p><h2><b>A new viewpoint</b></h2><p>Christiane Jalbert, a professor of classics, was among those who noted down the book's name. “I immediately thought: I need to read this,” she told <i>The National</i>. She said she had written her thesis on how 19th century French explorers described Asian men in their travel books. What appeared to draw her to Al Tahtawi was that he offered a reversal in perspective. </p><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/NDYT3VH4MZBOLED7AEELTUFHBM.jpg?auth=c564572d5cd4f9d2007539e3b4b0884d1d0f15b4da1f51662f63d1b2142c7a60&smart=true&width=5070&height=3380" alt="Neighbourhoods in the north of Paris are heavily associated with North African migration. Getty Images" height="3380" width="5070"/><p>Lydia Devige, a professor of French literature currently working in Egypt, was also curious. “It is absolutely true: France is a complete paradox. They claim to look after the poor, yet they still house them in institutions in 2026. [In Egypt] they have a real sense of community,” she said.</p><p>Some participants in the tour had expected to walk the streets of Barbes, in the north of Paris, where there is a high concentration of <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/mena/2025/12/25/algeria-demands-an-apology-and-reparations-from-france-for-its-crime-of-colonisation/" target="_blank" rel="" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/mena/2025/12/25/algeria-demands-an-apology-and-reparations-from-france-for-its-crime-of-colonisation/">North African</a> immigrants, shops and music. “We had no concept of Arab culture within Western culture prior to the migrations of the 1960s,” David Victor Basello, a craft artist, said.</p><p>Yet the tour showed that the history reaches much further back. Some 500 years ago, the College de France was founded in the Latin Quarter by a Renaissance king keen for Arabic to be taught in his kingdom. “That was a total discovery,” Mr Basello said. “I never thought the visit would go that far back in time.”</p><h2><b>Not a pastiche</b></h2><p>The tour began at the Arab World Institute, conceived in the post-colonial era of the 1960s, when France was thinking of ways to maintain relations with its former North African colonies. The project stalled before being revived after <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/mena/2026/07/24/the-deepest-gulf-oil-shocks-in-history-visualised/" target="_blank" rel="" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/mena/2026/07/24/the-deepest-gulf-oil-shocks-in-history-visualised/">the 1973 oil shock</a>, which triggered French interest in projecting soft power to the Gulf states. </p><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/ZLO7JC4MC5AS7BESHHWK2YYGXE.jpg?auth=69719cb04e6f4956ef46bb45c5ae10179867f12e6af5f6f6321c8edf7d2827a5&smart=true&width=4546&height=3025" alt="The College de France in Paris. AFP" height="3025" width="4546"/><p>France’s president at the time, Valéry Giscard d’Estaing, brought together France and 22 Arab states to create the institute, which has since been jointly managed by a French and Arab leadership structure, with an Arab director and a French president. </p><p>Opened in 1987, the institute was designed to blend into the Paris skyline, with a grey-and-glass facade featuring 240 metal panels inspired by traditional <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/arts-culture/2025/10/13/louvre-abu-dhabi-exhibit-sheds-light-on-beauty-of-shadows-from-arabian-mashrabiya-to-japanese-shoji-screens/" target="_blank" rel="" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/arts-culture/2025/10/13/louvre-abu-dhabi-exhibit-sheds-light-on-beauty-of-shadows-from-arabian-mashrabiya-to-japanese-shoji-screens/">Middle Eastern mashrabiya</a> latticework, commonly seen in the medieval quarters of Arab cities. “The idea was to create a very neutral building,” Ms Nida said. “Building a pastiche of Oriental architecture in the heart of Paris was not the goal.”</p><p>The panels open and close like the iris of an eye, responding to the intensity of the sun and helping to keep the light inside the building at a constant level. More traditional wooden mashrabiya could be seen later in the tour at the Great Mosque of Paris, after a two-and-a-half-hour walk.</p><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/CR4RUUNPQRDMLD5FW4G3ICESKU.jpg?auth=1c2a7575fda661db0d90e2518fb8bc66cb82d27149a4164d7f6d4dd4e8e30b62&smart=true&width=6000&height=4000" alt="Saint-Julien-le-Pauvre Church was converted into a Melkite Greek Catholic church in the 19th century. AFP" height="4000" width="6000"/><p>But the first stop was at the lesser-known 12<sup>th</sup> century church of Saint-Julien-le-Pauvre, built around the same time as the Notre-Dame cathedral. Closed during the French Revolution, it was later used for storage by the nearby Hotel-Dieu Hospital and was slated for demolition in the 19<sup>th</sup> century during Georges-Eugene Haussmann’s renovation of Paris. It was saved by writers and intellectuals such as Victor Hugo, who argued it had to be preserved because it was the oldest in Paris (a title in reality held by the Church of Saint-Germain-des-Pres).</p><h2><b>Levantine merchants</b></h2><p>A growing community of<a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/mena/2026/03/30/syria-christians-al-shara/" target="_blank" rel="" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/mena/2026/03/30/syria-christians-al-shara/"> Syrian and Lebanese Christians,</a> many of whom had moved to Paris to flee Ottoman persecution, offered to take it over. It became a Melkite Greek Catholic church with additions including an iconostasis, a decorated screen behind which the priest performs part of the liturgy. </p><p>“These Christians had heard of France – Napoleon Bonaparte’s expedition had resonated widely – and many viewed it not only as a Christian nation but also as the land of human rights and the French Revolution. They sought refuge in France, specifically in this neighbourhood – a wretched, dilapidated, and unsanitary area – to open shops, as many of them were merchants,” Ms Nida said.</p><p>At a brisk pace under the late-summer rain, Ms Nida then headed with her group for the College de France. In the 13<sup>th</sup> century, Paris witnessed the rise of universities as a space of learning at roughly the same time as the return of crusaders, Ms Nida said – “a pivotal moment for the realisation of the Eastern world's technical and scientific superiority over Europe”. </p><p>While it appeared clear to many that learning Arabic was essential to access Arab advances in science and medicine, resistance was fierce. The French clergy was particularly alarmed by the number zero, which represented emptiness and the devil. “This mistrust of the East ran quite deep; it affected not only Arabs but also Greek speakers. This all changed with the Renaissance and Francis the First,” who ruled from 1515 to 1547, Ms Nida said. </p><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/HPMH3AJM3ZBS5ETAO6AQKN2QIE.jpg?auth=06af7ee50a2e1e4f37b2aae65726fe133beacbfb82b8456b81c2eed9968d3356&smart=true&width=1152&height=648" alt="Francis the First, left, wanted better translators to communicate with Suleiman the Magnificent, right " height="648" width="1152"/><p>Frustrated that he could not find translators to speak to his ally, Ottoman ruler Soleiman the Magnificent, and eager to develop a network of diplomats fluent in local languages, in 1526 Francis the First created a school in Paris where students could study Arabic, and later Turkish and Persian. To this day, the College de France is known for its openness to new fields of study – it has just under 50 research chairs that constantly evolve.</p><h2><b>Competing impulses</b></h2><p>The audience was more familiar with the tour’s last stop, the <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/weekend/2022/02/25/inside-paris-grand-mosque-a-refuge-for-frances-muslims-for-close-to-a-century/" target="_blank" rel="" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/weekend/2022/02/25/inside-paris-grand-mosque-a-refuge-for-frances-muslims-for-close-to-a-century/">Great Mosque of Paris</a>, a landmark monument inspired by the architecture of Al Qarawiyyin mosque in Fes, Morocco. The mosque is famous among visitors for its restaurant, which serves pastries and mint tea. With the sun finally down, this “garden of Eden” was an ideal setting for explanations about how traditional Arab gardens are created to stimulate the five senses, with flowers, fruit trees, water and birds.</p><p>Construction began in the years after the First World War as a symbolic tribute to the nearly 100,000 Muslims from French colonies in North and West Africa who died fighting for France. At the time, there were very few mosques in France and Muslim soldiers were initially fed non-halal meat and buried according to Christian rites. </p><p>But the idea of building a mosque predated the war and was driven by two competing impulses: that of so-called Orientalists, who idealised the Middle East, and that of domestically focused politicians who wanted greater control over France’s Muslim population, according to Ms Nida. </p><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/4H5BJE3SWBEH3NLMXWCODQV44I.jpg?auth=85a5b48e9f8e87e5fd050eaead063aff80ad4d8e83a73517fc354c39bfc5facc&smart=true&width=6048&height=4024" alt="The Great Mosque of Paris was built shortly after the First World War. AFP" height="4024" width="6048"/><p>It was almost immediately criticised after its construction by anti-colonial activists and remains a symbol of historically fraught French-Algerian relations. </p><p>The mix of elegant architecture, portent and history, as well as the lived experience of so many, is well explored during the walking tour. It is a reminder that France’s relationship with the Arab and Islamic world has never been straightforward. But the connections endure and continue to shape Paris today.</p>]]></content:encoded><media:content url="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/4H25AIB3N5GUDAK7A332HQ2HDI.jpg?auth=750e7be3d882c2917123a6552edcdf3a61d3e8746be1c0faa1ff99c9ef6a733b&amp;smart=true&amp;width=5600&amp;height=3150" type="image/jpeg" height="3150" width="5600"><media:description type="plain"><![CDATA[The Great Mosque of Paris's 'garden of Eden' is designed to stimulate the five senses. AFP]]></media:description><media:credit role="author" scheme="urn:ebu">J-F ROLLINGER</media:credit></media:content></item><item><title><![CDATA[Saturday afternoon in Hebron: Two faces of Israeli occupation in a shared holy site]]></title><link>https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/mena/2026/09/18/hebron-ibrahimi-mosque-abraham-jews-palestine-israel/</link><guid isPermaLink="true">https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/mena/2026/09/18/hebron-ibrahimi-mosque-abraham-jews-palestine-israel/</guid><dc:creator><![CDATA[Hamza Hendawi]]></dc:creator><description></description><pubDate>Fri, 18 Sep 2026 18:00:00 +0000</pubDate><content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>Palestinian women wait patiently for the red light on a metal panel ahead to turn green so they can pass through a turnstile, one at a time. Next is a metal detector, a baggage scanner and a diligent search by female Israeli soldiers. </p><p>The women are not visiting loved ones incarcerated in a maximum-security jail. They are there to visit an ancient mosque in the occupied <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/mena/2026/06/19/fears-over-new-israeli-land-grabs-in-hebron-and-jerusalem/">West Bank city of Hebron</a>, which is home to the tombs of several prophets revered by Muslims and Jews, including Abraham and his wife Sarah. </p><p>If there is one place in Palestine where the story of occupation can be seen in a single frame, it is the Ibrahimi Mosque's compound.</p><p>Palestinians and Israelis, Muslims and Jews, pray metres apart, but live in two different realities, with barriers cutting through the holy site to separate worshippers and direct them down different paths. For Palestinians, reaching the mosque means navigating checkpoints, turnstiles and security searches under the watch of Israeli soldiers. For Israelis, the route is shorter, and access is far easier.</p><p>“It's like gaining access to a military base, not a mosque,” Youssef Al Jaabari, a member of <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/uk/2026/04/28/twinning-movement-boosts-links-between-palestinian-and-uk-cities/">Hebron's city council</a>, told <i>The National </i>during a recent tour of the compound. “Muslim visitors go through one search after another. These measures are unnecessary.” </p><p>Hebron's old city is recognised as a Palestinian World Heritage site. ​Hundreds of Jewish settlers live among tens of thousands of Palestinians in parts of the ancient city that are under Israeli security control. Their coexistence within the confines of the city's old quarter fuels tension and sometimes violent clashes.</p><p>Jews believe the Cave of the Patriarchs is where Abraham, Isaac, Jacob and their wives are buried. Muslims, who, like Christians, also revere Abraham, built the Ibrahimi mosque, also known as the Sanctuary of Abraham, in the ​14th century.</p><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/645S7A6B6JFVZC2KDF42BTRFUI.jpeg?auth=05042ae0f467fc9a87245f741d88b683388f965b8be38c82fdfe8cf57ddf6cde&smart=true&width=5712&height=4284" alt="The old city in Hebron, in the occupied West Bank. Hamza Hendawi / The National" height="4284" width="5712"/><p>The site is one of the most potent symbols of Israel's heavy-handed occupation at a time when settler violence and land grabs are fuelling tension in a region <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/mena/2026/09/13/israels-election-polls-are-stable-but-the-country-is-not/">occupied by Israel</a> for nearly 60 years. It is broadly comparable to the strict security measures enforced by Israeli authorities at Al Aqsa Mosque in Jerusalem.</p><p>But few places capture Hebron's contradictions so starkly.</p><p>Those who approached the tomb of Abraham on that Saturday afternoon could clearly hear Jewish worshippers chanting their prayers in the adjacent section of the mosque, which they have access to pray in. A closer look through a barred window in the room housing the tomb allows pilgrims on the Muslim side to glimpse the Jewish worshippers in their off-white shawls and skullcaps.</p><p>The sharing of the mosque's complex between Jews and Muslims follows a delicate schedule. Muslims can have the entire place to themselves for 10 days a year, covering major holidays and select days in the final third of the holy month of Ramadan. </p><p>The Jews also have 10 days every year when they can have exclusive access to the entire complex to mark special days in the Jewish calendar.</p><p>“We remove the carpets before the Jews take over our part of the mosque,” said Arafat Abu Turky, a veteran of seven years on the mosque's staff of caretakers.</p><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/WBWEZUX5HAGYRNI3VE6TNZ24ZU.jpg?auth=fef742fdb960509d82ab015c17491e55e89d0f1b1ff672331c2aaa3757050294&smart=true&width=6000&height=4000" alt="Palestinian students cross an Israeli checkpoint as they return to school for the new academic year, near Hebron. Reuters" height="4000" width="6000"/><p>Mr Al Jaabari said a US-mediated protocol on Hebron dating back to 1997 gave the city council full control over Hebron's old city and religious sites. </p><p>However, last June<a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/mena/2026/04/09/israel-secretly-approves-34-new-settlements-in-occupied-west-bank/"> Israel tightened its control</a> of the site, seizing planning and construction powers at the Ibrahimi mosque from Palestinian authorities, thus scrapping parts of the agreement, according to the Finance Minister, Bezalel Smotrich.</p><h2><b>'It is not right'</b></h2><p>Most of the mosque's patrons on a recent weekend afternoon were women, a telltale sign of the difficulties that face adult Palestinian males who, to get to Hebron, must negotiate the seemingly endless security checkpoints across the West Bank and the suspicion with which Israeli soldiers view them.</p><p>Yet the atmosphere inside the mosque was one of jubilation. A large group of women from the nearby city of Bethlehem were in the mosque to celebrate four of them for having learnt the entire Quran by heart, an undertaking that can take years. The four took turns reciting parts of the Quran as the rest of the women formed a circle around them.</p><p>“It is not right that I, as a Palestinian whose family is from Hebron, should go through these security checks to visit the mosque,” said one of the women, who did not want to be named for fear of reprisals. </p><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/D6RGWHBXS3YY3MEGGJXG4ECSEQ.jpg?auth=4d37e5369a2864d8345e3295a81d384b741586970bb369b719b720620dd7dc40&smart=true&width=5290&height=3543" alt="Israeli forces keep watch during Israeli settlers' weekly tour in Hebron. Reuters" height="3543" width="5290"/><p>“I was once denied entry here because my mobile phone's cover had a map of Palestine with the words 'Jerusalem is ours',” said the woman, who is a PhD candidate in education and came to the mosque with her mother, who runs a women's Quranic school in Bethlehem.</p><p>In another corner of the complex, a large group of Turkish Muslims sat on the floor, listening to their Palestinian tour guide explain, in fluent Turkish, the site's history and significance. Palestinian children found another way to pass the time in the mosque: excitedly chasing each other or playing hide-and-seek around the shrines, bookshelves, and the ornate pulpit.</p><p><a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/mena/2026/09/07/israeli-military-ordered-to-prepare-for-all-out-war-on-palestinian-authority/">Israeli soldiers</a> occasionally stage security raids in the Muslim part of the complex. Israeli security cameras installed throughout the section provide round-the-clock monitoring.</p><p>Mr Al Jaabari, the Hebron city council member, disputes the legality of the mosque's Jewish-Muslim sharing arrangement, claiming it was unilaterally introduced by an Israeli commission that had no mandate to do so.</p><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/PGQGX75OKZBOJE4DETFTESQJ2Q.jpeg?auth=83c1787acf52a5009d4fd92b60dfb5175773072a80e77ddf390519525b45be1d&smart=true&width=5712&height=4284" alt="The tomb of the Prophet Abraham in Hebron in the occupied West Bank. Hamza Hendawi / The National" height="4284" width="5712"/><p>“The Israelis have absolutely no rights in or a legitimate claim to the mosque. It's internationally recognised as a Muslim heritage site,” he said. “They are trying to change its Islamic character by adding new structures. The parties they organise at the mosque during their annual 10 days of full access are an offence to the feelings of Muslims.”</p><p>In a recent speech marking the establishment of a new Israeli settlement ​near Hebron, Mr Smotrich said the “historic step” of taking over the construction and planning of the site from the Palestinians would deepen “Israeli sovereignty” in the West Bank, which Palestinians seek as part of a <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/mena/2026/09/04/future-of-palestinian-cause-could-depend-on-november-legislative-elections-if-they-go-ahead/">future independent state</a>, together with the Gaza Strip.</p><p>According to Mr Al Jaabari, the Israeli authorities have been breaching the accord to pander to settlers in both the city and the rest of the West Bank. “The general election next month has increased and sped up the facilities provided by Israeli authorities for Hebron's settlers,” he warned.</p>]]></content:encoded><media:content url="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/CEPCCFKBDZFDDCZ57V264ETPFI.jpeg?auth=95f1a3ba374e9b8f60c0b6f5ab9d4a4052d3febedfd60995b2734f197c6d1163&amp;smart=true&amp;width=5712&amp;height=4284" type="image/jpeg" height="4284" width="5712"><media:description type="plain"><![CDATA[Palestinian women waiting to enter the Al Ibrahimi mosque in Hebron in the occupied West Bank. Hamza Hendawi/ The National]]></media:description></media:content></item><item><title><![CDATA[Would a new Israeli government be any different?]]></title><link>https://www.thenationalnews.com/opinion/comment/2026/09/18/israel-elections-politics-middle-east/</link><guid isPermaLink="true">https://www.thenationalnews.com/opinion/comment/2026/09/18/israel-elections-politics-middle-east/</guid><dc:creator><![CDATA[Matthew Gould]]></dc:creator><description></description><pubDate>Fri, 18 Sep 2026 18:00:00 +0000</pubDate><content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>When I was the British ambassador in <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/tags/israel" target="_self" rel="" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/tags/israel">Israel</a>, my number-one rule for Israeli politics was this – never bet against <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/tags/benjamin-netanyahu" target="_self" rel="" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/tags/benjamin-netanyahu">Bibi</a>.</p><p>Israeli Prime Minister <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/mena/2026/09/17/netanyahu-seeks-law-revoking-israeli-citizenship-of-those-who-smear-army/">Benjamin Netanyahu</a> is a brilliant and <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/mena/2026/09/02/netanyahu-vows-no-retreat-from-gaza-after-terrifying-israeli-commando-raid/">ruthless political operator</a>, with an extraordinary ability to snatch victory from the jaws of apparently inevitable defeat. In the past 17 years, he has been the country’s Prime Minister for all but 18 months. For much of the world, he is Israel, not just its leader but the <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/arts-culture/film-tv/2026/09/15/israel-gaza-naza-venice-film-yuval-abraham-rachel-szor/">personification of the country</a>. </p><p>On October 27, Israelis will vote in a general election for the 120-seat Knesset. Polling has been volatile all summer; the opposition parties led for most of it, but that lead has now gone. The latest polls put both blocs in the low to mid 50s, with Mr Netanyahu’s bloc ahead in several, and the remaining dozen or so seats going to two largely Arab parties. On most readings, no bloc reaches the 61 seats needed for a majority. </p><p>The politics will not stop with the vote but move into coalition-building. Mr Netanyahu will try to peel off opposition parties, promising ministerial jobs and the jettisoning of the far right in his current coalition. His opponents will look for the least difficult route to 61 seats, probably through defections or a coalition with the Islamist Ra'am party.</p><p>One way or another, Israel may well end up being led by a different government, and quite possibly a different prime minister. But how much difference will this actually make? A different prime minister will change some things and present a more palatable face to the world, but the core dilemmas facing Israel will remain unchanged.</p><p>The frontrunner to replace Mr Netanyahu is currently Gadi Eisenkot, the former chief of staff of the Israeli army, whose Yashar party has spent the summer running level with, or just ahead of, Mr Netanyahu’s Likud. Mr Eisenkot is in many respects the opposite of Mr Netanyahu: a working-class son of Moroccan immigrants, sober where Mr Netanyahu is dramatic, and widely respected, not least because he lost his son Gal in <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/tags/gaza">Gaza</a>, during an operation to recover the bodies of Israeli hostages in December 2023. Also in contention would be Naftali Bennett, who is a popular choice for prime minister despite his party trailing Mr Eisenkot’s in the polls.</p><p>It would be a mistake to assume that not being Mr Netanyahu makes either of his rivals peaceniks. Mr Eisenkot is widely recognised as the author of the Dahiya doctrine – the use of disproportionate force against civilian infrastructure in areas held by terrorist groups. He would come into office holding strong national security credentials and a clear set of positions on the key issues that provide a guide to where he would take Israeli policy. Across the range of those issues, he has disagreed with Mr Netanyahu about tactics and implementation but not about the core direction.</p><p>On Gaza, Mr Eisenkot has called for the elimination of Hamas and its replacement by an upgraded Palestinian Authority. Israel has haemorrhaged international support over the war, but the country remains traumatised by the attack on October 7, 2023, and there would be little support for any policy that looked soft on Hamas. Mr Eisenkot might avoid some of the more extreme statements and actions of the Netanyahu government, but the underlying dilemma remains – although most Israelis don’t want to permanently occupy the Gaza Strip, they have learnt that neither can they simply leave and hope for the best, and that there are few good options in between.</p><blockquote><p>Israel’s centre has historically been more forward on Palestinian statehood than the right, but even that distinction barely holds up in this election</p></blockquote><p>The same dilemma holds on Lebanon, where Israelis have bad memories of the “Lebanese mud” in which Israelis felt stuck during the 18-year occupation that ended in 2000. Many feel, however, that without such an occupation the Israeli state cannot guarantee the safety of its citizens living in the north.</p><p>Iran was always top of Mr Netanyahu’s agenda. He has warned about Tehran’s nuclear ambitions for more than three decades, and helped push US President Donald Trump to act. Yet despite the issue being personal for the Prime Minister, and deep disappointment among the Israeli public regarding the outcome of the conflict so far, there is little daylight between the leading contenders for prime minister – all call for continued pressure on Iran but disagree on the “how”. As army chief, Mr Eisenkot called for greater intelligence-sharing with Saudi Arabia. Mr Bennett has gone even further, calling for automatic retaliation against Iran in the case of any attack by Tehran’s proxy in Lebanon, <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/tags/hezbollah">Hezbollah</a>.</p><p>Israel’s centre has historically been more forward on Palestinian statehood than the right, but even that distinction barely holds up in this election. Mr Eisenkot’s stance reflects the widespread loss of faith among the Israeli public in the prospects for peace. “I have never said two states for two peoples,” he stated in June, adding that “anyone who talks about 'peace now' and two states doesn’t understand the conflict”.</p><p>Mr Bennett has said he will not allow a Palestinian state to be established, and on settlements he is potentially no more restrained than Mr Netanyahu – he has said that Area C of the <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/tags/west-bank">West Bank</a>, a zone that covers about 60 per cent of the territory and is under full Israeli security and administrative control, should ultimately become part of Israel. Mr Bennett once ran the settler movement’s umbrella body, the Yesha Council.</p><p>If there is a change, it is more likely to be at home and in tone rather than on the big security issues. Both Mr Eisenkot and Mr Bennett have called for an independent inquiry into the failures that allowed the October 7 attack to happen, one of the key issues of the campaign so far, on which feelings have been sharpened by the reporting of warnings received before the attack. Both have also called for the preservation of judicial independence. Both have backed drafting ultra-Orthodox men into the army. Mr Bennett has called for repairing frayed relations with the Democratic Party in the US, with European governments and with Jewish communities around the world.</p><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/74WZIBHEN6XKUTP7PKO3R6TT7U.jpg?auth=1055cab4dff57992489161cc13299e384f1e474b8622e0f486eae8a67b88d3ba&smart=true&width=6000&height=4000" alt="Election banners on display during a demonstration by ultra-Orthodox Jews protesting against mandatory recruitment into the Israeli military in Bnei Brak, August 24, 2006. EPA" height="4000" width="6000"/><p>For the Arab world, a new Israeli leader would represent an opportunity, but perhaps not for profound change. Mr Eisenkot has spoken of closer intelligence ties with Saudi Arabia; Mr Bennett wants to expand the Abraham Accords and pursue Saudi ties. Both might be easier for Arab governments to deal with than Mr Netanyahu, whose reputation and coalition partners have become a liability. However, Riyadh has made normalisation conditional on a credible path to Palestinian statehood – and neither Mr Eisenkot nor Mr Bennett is offering one. A change of prime minister would not remove that obstacle.</p><p>All of this means even if my number-one rule no longer holds, and Mr Netanyahu is finally forced out of the Prime Minister’s residence, what follows may be less different than many believe.</p>]]></content:encoded><media:content url="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/D5YGPYKBRFG2BPR4QCCA22BTXQ.jpg?auth=dc6584e8c73dda9358749fafe98d6e1f61ff33cccf98da93c2451567c34cdc24&amp;smart=true&amp;width=2520&amp;height=1417" type="image/jpeg" height="1417" width="2520"><media:description type="plain"><![CDATA[AFP / Reuters / The National]]></media:description></media:content></item><item><title><![CDATA[Havana's only mosque is a haven for Cuba's small Muslim community]]></title><link>https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/us/2026/09/18/habana-vieja-havana-cuba-abdallah-mosque/</link><guid isPermaLink="true">https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/us/2026/09/18/habana-vieja-havana-cuba-abdallah-mosque/</guid><dc:creator><![CDATA[Sara Ruthven]]></dc:creator><description></description><pubDate>Fri, 18 Sep 2026 18:00:00 +0000</pubDate><content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>The Habana Vieja neighbourhood is the centrepiece of the Cuban capital's normally <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/business/property/2026/06/17/cuba-turns-to-foreign-capital-to-rescue-collapsing-tourism-industry/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/business/property/2026/06/17/cuba-turns-to-foreign-capital-to-rescue-collapsing-tourism-industry/">bustling tourist industry</a>. Colourful colonial buildings house hotels, restaurants, souvenir shops and boutiques – and also, a mosque.</p><p>Sitting on a quiet side street, across from the city's Arab history museum, the <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/gulf-news/saudi-arabia/2022/09/19/saudi-arabia-to-fund-cubas-first-mosque-ambassador-says/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/gulf-news/saudi-arabia/2022/09/19/saudi-arabia-to-fund-cubas-first-mosque-ambassador-says/">Abdallah Mosque</a> caters to Havana's small Muslim community. </p><p>“This is a touristic area and when we used to have more tourism, they [foreign Muslim visitors] would come a lot,” Aqil Alexis Sanchez Tamayo, one of the imams at Abdallah Mosque, tells <i>The National.</i> “And they were always shocked at finding a mosque in <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/tags/cuba" target="_blank" rel="" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/tags/cuba">Cuba</a>.”</p><p>The mosque opened in 2015 in what was previously a classic car museum. A team from <a href="https://www.thenationalnews.com/tags/saudi-arabia" target="_blank" rel="" title="https://www.thenationalnews.com/tags/saudi-arabia">Saudi Arabia</a> worked with Cubans to design the interior of the building, which can hold about 2,000 people.</p><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/4NEWBFFUN5GIBOR2N5MI7U4ZBM.png?auth=0e5bd5d08bc2977cadcfcd755db0aff796e351ccd6d33637db63310218e0cf14&smart=true&width=3416&height=1780" alt="A plaque outside the mosque in Havana, which is located in a normally busy tourist district. Ahmed Issawy / The National" height="1780" width="3416"/><p>“Since Ramadan of 2015, it became … the neurological centre of the Islamic community in Cuba,” Mr Sanchez Tamayo says.</p><p>Cuba is a communist country that discourages public displays of religion, but a landmark agreement between Havana and Saudi Arabia in 2022 paved the way for the mosque's construction. Saudi Arabia was the first Arab country to establish formal diplomatic relations with Cuba in 1956.</p><p>Before Abdallah opened, there was only a small mosque available to diplomats and other foreigners living in Havana, in the Casa del Arabe, the Arab history museum. The new mosque serves Cuba's entire Muslim community and welcomes visitors from abroad. </p><p>Cuba's Muslim community numbers only about 8,000, Mr Sanchez Tamayo says, a mix of Cuban converts and resident foreigners. He himself converted to Islam more than 20 years ago and studied in Turkey, and served in the leadership of the Islamic League of Cuba as well as becoming one of the imam's of the mosque. </p><p>“We have a lot of students from abroad, diplomatic personnel, people who base it here in Cuba, who practise Islam,” he says. </p><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/IB7MHDQBUFCFHO3BASYWSDVQK4.png?auth=876e4d1d40e51d5d06dbaae42230182cefa52dd39a39dcd56629a3ebe7b0a1e6&smart=true&width=3428&height=1808" alt="Cuba's Muslim community numbers only about 8,000. Ahmed Issawy / The National" height="1808" width="3428"/><p>Abdallah Mosque is served by three imams – two from Palestine and Mr Sanchez Tamayo – and they take turns leading Friday prayers.</p><p>Mr Sanchez Tamayo says that another, larger mosque in the area is being planned, also with support from Riyadh. </p><p>“It will be in this area of the harbour of Havana, near to the power plant that used to be one of the more important of Havana, but it will take some time,” he explains. “It will be bigger than this one, and with more accommodations and services than this one.”</p><p>The Saudi embassy in Havana did not respond to a request for comment.</p><p>For Mr Sanchez Tamayo, the mosque is a testament to the resiliency of the Cuban people in the face of mounting challenges. </p><p>Tourism, once the economic lifeblood of the island, slowed dramatically after the Covid pandemic and even further under mounting US sanctions. Scarcity of fuel, food and other necessities have made life “stressful”, he says.</p><p>“We are suffering, but being Muslim, we are more than aware that every single thing that happens in our life is decreed by Allah, and we are trying to have that level of resilience to overcome this situation.”</p>]]></content:encoded><media:content url="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/DDISDJFKEJC2LER2TS4VVGL5MY.png?auth=39be9493fe0bede7d03725e7e315fba594e4bc0987db47999f1d77c75337a2ae&amp;smart=true&amp;width=3430&amp;height=1790" type="image/png" height="1790" width="3430"><media:description type="plain"><![CDATA[Havana's Abdallah Mosque was founded in 2015. Ahmed Issawy / The National]]></media:description></media:content></item><item><title><![CDATA[How western veterans helped the UAE build one of the region's most adept military forces]]></title><link>https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/uae/2026/09/18/uae-armed-forces-expats-western-military-veterans-history/</link><guid isPermaLink="true">https://www.thenationalnews.com/news/uae/2026/09/18/uae-armed-forces-expats-western-military-veterans-history/</guid><dc:creator><![CDATA[Daniel Bardsley]]></dc:creator><description></description><pubDate>Fri, 18 Sep 2026 18:00:00 +0000</pubDate><content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>For more than six decades, since before the UAE’s founding, thousands of western veterans have served alongside Emirati forces, helping build a formidable military and producing a force regarded as one of the region’s most capable.</p><p>The UAE has a tradition of hiring residents to train and work alongside its own military personnel. Pakistan has long been a handy source of expertise but western military experts have been stationed alongside Emirati forces for just as long. </p><p>Over the decades, more than 5,000 western military expatriates have served or worked alongside the UAE Armed Forces, with an estimated 80 per cent from the UK or the US, according to a new book. <i>Western Military Expatriates in the Armed Forces of the UAE: History and Impact, 1965–2024</i>, shines a spotlight on these foreign personnel. Written by Dr Athol Yates, an associate professor of international and civil security at Khalifa University in Abu Dhabi, it features case studies from the early days through to the 2020s.</p><h2><b>Different culture</b></h2><p>Among them is Adrian Raisbeck, 53, a Malawi-born former British Royal Marine who came to the UAE in the early 2000s to analyse maritime security. He found himself operating in a very different cultural environment from the one he was used to.</p><p>As with most westerners, he was not part of the formal chain of command, so to become effective he had to develop the influence he could have on others.</p><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/3GSDC4MALJG5VN45ITT7HBXPAY.tif?auth=bcd1b57822a164bad5f3c5fa3300cc9d84f994d0e433cc48d79ae4f3089e737e&smart=true&width=2824&height=2097" alt="Ruler of Abu Dhabi meeting the all-British leadership of his military in 1968 Photo: Dr Athol Yates" height="2097" width="2824"/><p>“After I’d been there for about eight weeks, I wanted to leave,” Mr Raisbeck, who served as a lieutenant colonel, said. “I couldn’t see how I was going to be able to cross these cultural divides in a way that would see me taking what I’d learnt in the Royal Marines, both culturally and technically, and convey that to an Emirati leadership body that couldn’t really speak English very well and had come from a very different background to me and seemed to prioritise, I think, personal relationships.”</p><p>A former senior British military officer working with the UAE military passed him a book by TE Lawrence – Lawrence of Arabia – that emphasised the importance of allowing Arab forces to do things their way.</p><p>“That was quite a seminal moment for me and instigated a complete mindset change from my side,” Mr Raisbeck said. “I did a bit more introspecting and decided I was going to build personal relationships with my Emirati colleagues. In order to do that I was going to need language, which meant I was going to have to learn a lot of Arabic.”</p><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/K75DXS6SDBFGTPEUY5I5NDNC5E.jpg?auth=67aa29c36e096e65b9c348a91145814666948f9b07d019a1701d41c3b0f4998c&smart=true&width=1200&height=799" alt="Another photo showing expatriates facilitate a briefing of UAE and US personnel for a training exercise at a combat training centre in Abu Dhabi Emirate Photo: Dr Athol Yates" height="799" width="1200"/><h2><b>Building bridges, forming friendships</b></h2><p>He started to do just that and, for months, would step into the offices of Emirati personnel to chat, build relationships and, in turn, create influence. </p><p>Mr Raisbeck and other British personnel developed such strong bonds that Emirati officers invited them to socialise at their homes.</p><p>“I made good friends in the process and once you’ve broken through that outer exterior, you can have a laugh together,” said Mr Raisbeck, who now runs a social and agricultural enterprise in Malawi. “This meant that when we were getting down to business and advising, we were, I hope, able to be more effective.”</p><p>Early on, residents helped “accelerate the development” of the armed forces of what became the UAE, Dr Yates said, and some were commissioned into the chain of command. “Rather than organically grow them, which would’ve taken 20 years, they could do that in a couple of years.”</p><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/DUTCUKKDBBDTVKUXFIEAQUU3SY.jpeg?auth=fae360bef2cdadcdd6f131e37c953600ceee0cf765a473528cb300dcd71cc62a&smart=true&width=1600&height=1071" alt="Lieutenant Colonel Adrian Raisbeck (right) with Lieutanant Colonel Paul Levey during their time in the UAE Photo: Adrian Raisbeck" height="1071" width="1600"/><p>The roles most western expats have played, he said, are best understood through a business analogy. “The expats are not employed in management, for example, the officer corps, nor in operations, for example, the soldier, but sit outside the organisation like consultants,” he said.</p><p>While they have often brought a specific capability that they have passed on to Emirati personnel, the necessity to continuously modernise the military and bring in new capabilities means that there remains a need for residents.</p><p>“They are advisers, trainers, system commissioners, maintainers,” Dr Yates said. “They’re not in the normal chain of command, with very few exceptions.” At least five western expats are known to have died while serving, some during air accidents.</p><p>However, the “vast majority very much enjoyed” their experiences in the UAE, Dr Yates said, often because they had a degree of autonomy that they did not have before.</p><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/WEQ6NYHVBRD3DF32XKWDVFU3UE.jpg?auth=46033e2798a1d2498ab46f1aafb24fc4c3a1ba2bc7c165537c56ebd52289401e&smart=true&width=3727&height=5600" alt="The one-page main employment contract for a British contract officer in the Abu Dhabi Defence Force, 1969 Photo: Dr Athol Yates" height="5600" width="3727"/><p>A former British Army officer who came to the UAE to train military helicopter pilots said it was important to allow Emirati personnel to develop their own ways of working, rather than trying to replicate the “rigid discipline” he was used to. He added that westerners who did not acknowledge this often struggled.</p><p>He said he found working with the UAE Armed Forces “a good learning experience”, in part because it made him question elements of the training he had once received.</p><h2><b>Steep learning curve</b></h2><p>Darren Boudreau, 56, a former British Royal Marine who came to teach boat operations and skills, said it was important not to be too much of a disciplinarian, as that wasn't effective in what was, for him, a different cultural context.</p><p>“We explained to them, ‘We’re here to teach you skills. We’re here to do a job so you can succeed in your job, and get home and feed your families,’” Mr Boudreau said. “That massively changed the landscape for us. All of a sudden they really started working with us.”</p><img src="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/5ALEONNZMBCL7B6G4G5JZC43J4.jpg?auth=e5487308e11f7e0b3dd48a618a8fd9652f6f74c86f4d5e4c13f562539c51be46&smart=true&width=640&height=480" alt="Major General Andrew Pillar meeting with an Emirati officer Photo: Andrew Pillar" height="480" width="640"/><p>He also found that learning to speak the language made a big difference.</p><p>One of the most senior military personnel to move to the UAE in recent times was Andrew Pillar, who left the British Royal Marines as a brigadier and, during his time in the UAE from 2002 to 2011, brought over many of his former colleagues from the Marines to run training.</p><p>He said it was “a steep learning curve” for many who moved to the Emirates, not least because – echoing others – they often had to realise that what had worked in the British Armed Forces may not be appropriate in the UAE.</p><p>To give them authority, even though they were not part of the formal chain of command, gave Brig Pillar, and many others, ranks. He was promoted to major general.</p><p>He said some Emirati personnel recognised the value they brought when introducing their ways of operating. “We were involved in every area of the forces,” he said. “There were a lot of Emirati officers reluctant to see us go.”</p>]]></content:encoded><media:content url="https://www.thenationalnews.com/resizer/v2/56IPPFEQLVBEHBSFNUWC5DHT44.jpg?auth=2550b222939a2d7871b4a908e4ee888c301df9d716c00def0d3df3a1e6b391e0&amp;smart=true&amp;width=5960&amp;height=3352" type="image/jpeg" height="3352" width="5960"><media:description type="plain"><![CDATA[The US Women’s Military Service Technical Assistance Field Team, 1992.
